Example for TIMER:

Timers 1 to 3 are used for defining time counters that are started as soon as a SET condition is
reached, running together with the program until a given time has expired.

If the current goes below 5 A, the battery is recharged in step 7 with 5 A until Timer2 has expired.

           If Timer expires before reaching step 7, the program leaves out this step und jumps to the
           next step.

           When the limit is a timer that can also accept negative values such as Ah or Wh, please
           observe the respective sign. An upper limit for the discharged capacity should be entered
           such as < - 100 Ah.

The following program example contains one pause step of 30 seconds and one charge step with
1 A charging current. To terminate the charge step, you will have to set another limit. For instance,
enter an upper voltage limit of 14.1 V in the respective column. The correct input is simply: > 14.1
V. As soon as the system measures a voltage of more than 14.1 V, the program will execute the
action indicated in the same program line. Please also refer to chapter 12.4.5.

Program example:

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 176 of 363
Special limits "A_notAbs" and "mA_notAbs"

A normal positive current limit does not take into account whether a current is positive or negative.

Example:

When charging, limit > 10 A causes an error (ERR 1) when current exceeds 10 A.

When discharging, limit > 10 A also leads to ERR 1 for currents like e.g. -12 A because a positive
current limit does not care if a current is negative or not and 12 is greater than 10.

With a defined negative limit value like < -20 A (see above) this problem does not occur.

With new limits A_notAbs (for currents in A) and mA_notAbs (for currents in mA) the described
problem with positive current limits can be avoided.

Example:

Limit A_notAbs ("not absolute") always respects if a current is positive or negative. So in the
second example, limit > 10 A_notAbs only reacts on positive currents that exceed 10 A.

Limits A_notAbs and mA_notAbs can be used as normal current limits. In column Limit, just use
"special units" A_notAbs and mA_notAbs instead of normal units A and mA.

GradT, GradI, GradU

           These limits are only usable after a device adjustment and only for a maximum of one
           channel. Please contact Digatron for details.

With these limits a step can be terminated if a given increase of temperature, current or voltage
occurs within an hour or minute (defined by adding an "m" to the function, e.g. GradUm).

Example:

The step is terminated on a rise of temperature of at least 2 degrees within one minute. Use a limit
with a negative sign to respond to the decrease of a value.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 177 of 363
The functions work like this:

Example: GradUm

At the beginning of the step, the present voltage Y is defined as reference point X (X=Y). Now for
one minute with each new registration value, i.e. every 100 ms, GradUm is calculated from the
recently measured voltage (Y) and the reference point (X) as follows:

GradUm = Y ­ X

With GradUm a new reference point (X) is defined every minute, i.e. X=Y and thus GradUm is
reset to 0.

With GradU a new reference point is defined only every hour.

The check of these limits takes place with the maximum measuring speed, i.e. every 100 ms.

Graphical representation GradU(m):

deltaV Limit with BTS-600

deltaV realizes the algorithm dChannel/dt for the standard voltage channel or for GREAL channels.

The following global variables are used by the routine:

GTIMER[1]
GREAL[100] = TimeIdx (ms)
GREAL[101] = ChanIdx
GREAL[102] = ChanNum

Standard usage:

With standard usage the global variables do not have to be used.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 178 of 363
The limit monitors the voltage at sense input.

Examples:      If the voltage is more than 0.5 Volts below maximum, the step is terminated.
< -0.5 deltaV  (Note: -0.7 is smaller than -0.5.)
               If the voltage is more than 1.5 Volts above maximum, the step is terminated.
> 1.5 deltaV

Enhanced Usage with Gradient Analysis:

The limit monitors the voltage at sense input.

If Time is set, this value is used as time base for the limit. Time is defined in ms and determines
the time between two measuring points.

Example:       If the rise of the channel is negative and smaller than 0.5, the function
< -0.5 deltaV  makes a break.

> 1.5 deltaV   If the rise of the channel is positive and greater than 1.5, the function makes
               a break.

Enhanced Usage for analyzing several GREAL channels:

The limit monitors GREAL channels 100 - 119 (GREAL[100] ­ GREAL[119]).

ChanIdx        defines which GREAL to start with
ChanNum        defines the number of channels

Battery Manager User Manual                     99.3                    Page 179 of 363
Example for using AhDef and PercAh:

AhDef is a limit function that saves the current Ah value as an absolute value (unsigned) in
GREAL [400] for processing using PercAh.

The PercAh limit function always refers to the AhDef value.

Let's say the Ah counter after step 3 is at 8 Ah. By using limit > 1 AhDef in step 4 this value is now
taken as (1 = 100%) nominal capacity.

Step 6 performs a current / voltage charging. When current reaches 1 A, it is kept constant until the
Ah counter reaches 130% of the value fixed by AhDef (> 130 PercAh in step 7) which is
1.3 x 8 Ah = 10.4 Ah.

Please note that Ah counter has to be reset in steps 2 and 5.

Example for using PERCCN_P:

PERCCN_P terminates a step when the capacity at the end of the previous step reached the
specified percentage of the nominal capacity. The nominal capacity of the battery has to be defined
in the battery dialog.

PERCCN_P and PERCCN_C have nothing to do with AhDef. Both functions work with the battery
value CNom and the Ah value, which should be appropriately assigned bevor the steps that want
to work with the functions.

           PERCCN_P does not take the sign of the Ah value into account.

Works the same as PercAh which relates to AhDef.

This step is terminated if the Ah counter at the end of the previous step reached 30%
(> 30 PERCCN_P) of the nominal capacity (e.g. 3 Ah when nominal capacity is 10 Ah).

Example for using PERCCN_C:

PERCCN_C terminates a step, when the specified percentage of the nominal capacity is reached
in the present step. The nominal capacity of the battery has to be defined in the battery dialog.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 180 of 363
This step is terminated as soon as the Ah counter reaches 40% (> 40 PERCCN_C) of the nominal
capacity (e.g. 4 Ah when nominal capacity is 10 Ah).

           PERCCN_C does not take the sign of the Ah value into account.

12.4.5 Action

The Action column indicates how a test step is terminated as soon as the associated limit is
reached.

The simplest case is that, when the limit is reached, the program will continue with the next test
step. In this case, the Action column will remain blank.

With certain battery conditions the test process may, however, require a carefully directed program
flow. The following actions are disposable:

Action            Description

Blank             When the associated limit is reached, the next test step will be executed.

INT               When the associated limit is reached, the program will be interrupted.

STO               When the associated limit is reached, the program will be stopped.

GOTO - Jump       When the limit is reached, the program will jump to the program step that
Destination       contains the entry Jump Destination in column Label.
Procedure Name
                  When the limit is reached, the procedure indicated here will be loaded. Then
                  the following program step will be activated. After having executed the
                  procedure, the program will go to the next program step.

Special Actions:

Action            Description

ERR               Message in registration data with interruption of the program. Please also
MSG               see chapter 12.4.7, example 11.

                  Message in registration data without interruption of the program. Please also
                  see chapter 12.4.7, example 11.

Battery Manager User Manual    99.3  Page 181 of 363
Actions have a fundamental effect on the test process. You should therefore read and observe the
following information very carefully:

An Interruption of the program (INT action) should be activated with unusual or dangerous battery
conditions, e.g. over-temperature, imminent over-charge, exhaustive discharge, etc. The test
process can be continued only after you have given the expressive command to do so. This can be
done either by actuating the "Start" button on the front panel of the test circuit or by entering
commands in the host computer (tool buttons or dispo list). Prior to giving this command, give your
battery a thorough check.

Stopping the program by entering the STO command in the Action column will merely stop the
actual test, but not the remaining tests indicated in the dispo list. If, for instance, a charge test is
followed by a discharge test and charging has been terminated for whatever reason, the discharge
test will start nevertheless. This may cause an exhaustive discharge!

           The STO action does not terminate the test process, but only the actual program! The
           system will execute further tests contained in the dispo list! If, due to premature
           interruption of the test, the battery could not be fully charged, the subsequent discharge
           test may lead to an exhaustive discharge!

GOTO Commands only apply to the same program level! It is, for instance, not possible to jump
from a procedure to the main menu or to another procedure!

If you enter a procedure in the action column you can promptly react to certain events or statuses
such as error handling, activating recharge procedures, etc.

How to use procedures and GOTO commands is described in paras 12.5.2 and 12.5.3.

Experience shows that it can be useful to enter a pause step at the end of a program:

This program is now ready for operation. It does, however, not yet produce sufficient registration
data. Though you did define, by entering the SET operator, which channels will be registered, you
have not yet defined the registration rate. This setting is made in the last column. It will be
described in the chapter 12.4.6.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 182 of 363
12.4.6 Registration

The last column of the program editor serves to define the frequency in which the registration data
are recorded. The frequency can be set individually for each test step.

If this column is left blank for a certain step, the registration data will, in any case, be registered at
the beginning and at the end of the test step. Please observe that the quantity of registered data
effects the time needed for subsequent test evaluations. Carefully consider which registration rate
to set and adapt it to your actual requirements.

BTS-600 offers many variations for registration. The following table will give you a survey on all
available registration formats. The quantities and counters listed in the table stand for all existing
registration channels.

 Registration   Example         Description
      Type
                2.0 A           registers when the current changes by 2 A
Delta           5 min           registers every 5 minutes
                < 0.5 A         registers when the current falls below 0.5 A
Threshold <, >  > 50.0 C        registers when the temperature exceeds 50 °C
                >> 1 h &        registers each voltage change by 2 V one hour after the
Range           2.0 V           beginning of the step
>> &

<< &            << -100.0 Ah &  registers each temperature change by 1 °C after a
Maximum *       1.0 C           100 Ah discharge
                15 *            registers exactly 15 times with the highest resolution of
                                the A/D converter, i.e. in intervals of 0.1 seconds

                >> 10000 Watt 5* registers 5 times with the highest resolution when a
                                           power of 10 kW is exceeded

                >> 30 sec 1*    registers once exactly 30 seconds after the begin of a
                                step

Special registrations:

Registration    Description

Registration    Only in connection with operators SET, CHA, DCH, RCH, PAU, REG.
Format Name     Defines the registration format used in a program.

You can, of course, also combine several of the above registration types in one test step.

The first type, "Delta", is the registration type used most frequently. If, for instance, you are
interested in obtaining an evaluation of the current curve, you will record whenever the current
changes by a certain value. It might also be important for you to know the exact time response.
You will then need registration data recorded in constant intervals.

You will have to determine for each test which registration data are of importance and enter the
registration setting correspondingly. We would, at any rate, recommend you to make economic use
of the registration function.

Battery Manager User Manual     99.3  Page 183 of 363
The following program example is a simple charge program with constant charge current. So it
would not make sense to have registrations with current fluctuations. What might be of interest is
the voltage curve during the charging process. Therefore, BTS-600 should record whenever the
voltage changes by 0.5 V. To set this registration function, move the cursor to the last column of
the third test step. Enter 0.5 V in this field.
As the voltage may remain constant for some time, enter an additional time registration (e.g. every
five minutes) to register the data measured in the meantime. Double-click the field with the
registration just created, perform a line break and enter 5 min.

If the registration column contains several entries for one test step, the sequence of the entries is
of no importance.

12.4.7 Program Examples

In the following you will find several examples describing the applications used most frequently in
battery testing.
The examples are applicable for operators DCH, CHA, and RCH. The battery will be charged or
discharged with constant current, voltage, power or resistance.

           To use units such as C, A, Ah, and h in programs, there must be channels with these
           units in the circuits used.

Example No. 1: Discharge
The following is a discharge step with constant current, which will be terminated as soon as the
voltage falls below a preset limit voltage. The registration will, above all, supply measuring points
for the voltage curve.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 184 of 363
Example No. 2: Charge in two steps
The battery will be charged with constant 20 A until it reaches a voltage of 14.1 V. Then BTS-600
regulates this voltage value, i.e. the charge current will be reduced while the charge operation
advances. The registration was specially defined for this current curve.

Example No. 3: Discharge in two steps
The first step ends when 50 Ah have been discharged.

           Discharge limits must be entered with a negative sign!

The second step contains a limit of the admissible battery temperature. When the temperature
exceeds that limit, the test will be interrupted. When the voltage falls below 10.5 V, the registration
rate for this step will be reduced from 5 minutes to 2 minutes.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 185 of 363
Example No. 4: Charge and Discharge
It often makes sense to combine charge steps (example No. 2) and discharge steps (example No.
3) in one program. In this case, you should, at any rate, reset the counters to quickly view the
charge level of the battery.

Example No. 5: Parallel Functions
Example No. 2 shows a charge program with two charge steps, which can also be combined in
one step. For this purpose, two nominal values are assigned to the CHA operator. These values
will be observed simultaneously, i.e. none of them may be exceeded. The battery will be charged
with maximal 20 A. During this process, the voltage can increase to maximum 14.1 V. When this
nominal value is reached, the voltage will be kept constant, i.e. the charge current decreased.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 186 of 363
Example No. 6: Programs with battery parameters
Using battery parameters allows you to use your programs for multifold applications. The following
example contains the same charge program as described in example No. 5, the nominal capacity
being 100 Ah and the battery being constructed of 6 cells. The current of 1.0 Acn5, supplied for five
hours, then equals 20 A, 0.05 Acn5 equals 1 A, a cell voltage of 2.35 V per cell equals 14.1 V
battery voltage.

Example No. 7: Ramp Function

Steps 3 and 4 contain discharges in ascending or descending ramp form. Apart from the initial and
the end value of the ramp, you will also have to define the ramp duration in the Nominal Value
column. The time values entered in the Limit column may not correspond with the ramp duration.

If the ramp is executed before the limit is elapsed (step 3), the battery will be discharged with the
ramp end value for the remaining time. Our example shows a one-minute ramp discharge, followed
by another minute discharge with 1000 watts.

If the limit elapses before the ramp is executed (step 4), the limit has priority. After 45 minutes, the
next test step will be executed. At that moment, the discharge current will be approx. 35 A.

Step 3 contains a voltage limit (<9.8 V) in the limit column, leading to a test stop (STO action). If
this voltage limit is reached in step 4, the test will not be stopped as step 5 merely contains a
pause.

This pause serves to monitor the voltage after the end of the discharge process.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 187 of 363
Example No. 8: Jump
If the test is stopped prematurely in step 3 of the previous example (STO with < 9.8 V), no
registration data will be recorded after the end of discharge. This can be avoided by using a GOTO
command. When the limit is reached, the program will be continued with the jump command, which
is "Pause" in our example. A jump command must be entered in the Label column. For further
information on jumps please see chapter 12.5.3.

Example No. 9: Interruption
The program is interrupted. To continue the program, either actuate the "START" button on the test
circuit or reactivate the program on the host computer (direct functions or tool buttons in the main
menu).

The program starts with an interruption. After the program is started on the host computer, it will
remain in hold state until you either actuate the "START" button on the test circuit or continue the
program on the host computer. The following charge will be interrupted when 14.0 volts are
reached. The program will have to be reactivated manually also with this interruption.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 188 of 363
Example No. 10: Actions in Test Steps
Actions defined in test steps serve to react with different actions on different limits. If a limit is
reached, the action set in the same line will be executed. No entry means: Go to next step.

The following example shows different limits with different actions.

Explanations on the Individual Limits and Actions:

                                         If the current reaches a value smaller than 0.5 amps, the charge
process will be terminated. The program will jump to the next step.

                                         If the charge process takes more than 10 hours, it will be
interrupted. An error message will be written to the registration data.

                             If the 100 Ah limit is reached prior to the 0.5 A limit, the program will

be interrupted.

                                         When the battery temperature rises to 40° C, a 1-hour pause will be
initiated. After elapse of this pause, the program will jump back to the charge process.

           A limit will always terminate the running step. There are only two exceptions to this rule,
           namely if MSG or ERR are set as actions.
           MSG will merely send a message to the registration data and to the host computer. It will
           not terminate the program step.
           ERR will send a message and interrupt the running step. You can have the program
           continued at the same position of the same step by actuating the "Start" button on the test
           circuit.

Certain actions only function if used with certain operators. Combined with general limits the SET
operator can send MSG or interrupt the current step with ERR.

For further information please refer to paras 12.4.8.5 and 12.4.8.6.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                     Page 189 of 363
Example No. 11: &-linked Limits

To link limits, enter an & sign after the limit. This links the limit in the next line with the preceding
limit. You can thus define complex conditions as limits.

The charge step will be terminated when the current is smaller than 0.5 amps, the step runtime has
reached 10 hours, and the Ah counter has exceeded 100.0.

           To trigger an action on an &-linked limit, the entry in the Action column must be at the
           height of the last limit condition.
           Limit channels collected by a unit (e.g. > 12 V1) are ANDed in connection with an
           &-linked limit.

12.4.8 How to Use Special Operators and Actions

This chapter contains supplementary explanations on the application of special program steps.

12.4.8.1 How to Set Variables

With the SET command you may define and set your proper variables. To do this, simply equate
the variable name to a certain value.

           If you use a variable name that is also a channel unit in the attribution circuit/channel,
           the according channel will be set to the value assigned to the variable. This may be
           advisable for presetting counters such as Ah and Wh.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 190 of 363
Why Variables?

You may use these variables wherever the corresponding values would usually appear in a
program. That is to say that, whenever you use values frequently in a program, you had better use
variables. If you change this variable, the program will automatically adapt each program step
containing this variable to the new value. You can also use these variables to preset cycle
counters. Please also see chapter 12.5.1.

In above figure the cycle counter is preset to value 543 and variable CurValue is defined and set to
value 20. Please note that "cycle" is a freely selected name, i.e. a variable. To assign this variable
to a cycle, you will have to enter the same variable name after the cycle beginning BEG. Setting
the GOTO command effects that the program jumps directly into the cycle. This avoids counter
resets. In our case, this effects that three cycles will be executed successively. Both the charge
and the discharge step will be executed with 20 amps, as the variable CurValue was set to 20.

12.4.8.2 How to Set Global Nominal Values or Limits

Global nominal values are limits that affect the entire program, that means each program step. The
corresponding values do not exceed or fall below these limits. If, for instance, this limit should be
reached with a certain watt value set in the program, the test circuit will automatically regulate the
power to this limit.

The same applies to global limits. A limit does, however, perform the action preset thereafter in the
same line. Possible actions are ERR (chapter 12.4.8.5) and MSG (chapter 12.4.8.6).

Please also refer to the explanations about operator CLEAR given in chapter 12.4.8.1010.

           A global limit without an action terminates the program as soon as it is reached.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 191 of 363
12.4.8.3 Registration as Program Step

The REG command will register once in the registration format entered in this line.
How to store a registration in a separate test section:

If you enter a name in the Nominal Value column, the registration initiated by the REG command
will be recorded in this test section. This allows writing certain results of all executed cycles
separately for quicker evaluation. You can, for instance, determine that only capacity values are
recorded in a separate test section at the end of each cycle. This enables you to display the
capacity curve of a battery as graph or in numerical figures without having to evaluate a large
amount of data that contains all registrations of a program.

If the REG operator is used without an entry in the Nominal Value column, the registration is
carried out with the registration format that was last defined with operator SET.

12.4.8.4 How to Use the "TASK" Operator

This operator activates a program running in parallel to the BTS program. This program serves to
control relays, temperatures or similar.

The TASK command allows you to integrate up to 12 parallel processes into the program. In the
above example the temperature of a water bath is controlled to 40 degrees Celsius. Please
consider that activation of this process is not possible without the appropriate hardware!

BTS-600 allows integrating a multitude of TASK enhancements. Please contact Digatron for
further information.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                                    Page 192 of 363

12.4.8.5 How to Use the "ERR" Operator

Error status with interruption of program.
ERR as independent test step:

The test run will be interrupted when the program arrives at the 8th test step. The "Error" LED and
the error relay output will be set on the test circuit. Beyond that, a message will be written into the
database together with registration data. The message is linked with the number entered in the
Nominal Value column.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                 Page 193 of 363
Which messages are available can be taken from the documentation belonging to the particular
delivery. By default there are these messages:

Number  Message
1       Limit reached!
2       Voltage limit reached!
3       Temperature limit reached!
4       Current limit reached!
5       Capacity limit reached!
6       External control signal activated!

ERR as action:
You can also set the ERR operator in column Action.

If the program is in step 4 for 5:30 hours, an interruption with error output will occur. The message
belonging to the number entered behind ERR will be written into the database together with
registration data.

           A single limit with ERR writes the message and goes into interrupt. After resuming, the
           next step is processed. If there are two limits in the step and the ERR limit is reached
           first, the message is written and after continuing it waits for the second limit to be
           reached.

           If there are two limits, it might happen that the step is left before the ERR limit is
           reached.

           Global limits behave like local limits in conjunction with ERR.

Create and edit messages
With menu entry Maintenance ­ BTS-600 Messages

The messages listed here are used by the associated number in conjunction with the operators
ERR and MSG.
You can change both numbers and message texts in this list. You can also add new messages by
double-clicking in a field in the empty line marked with an asterisk * and then entering the number
and the message text next to it.

As soon as you leave the new line, the entry is saved.
You can also delete entries by right-clicking in the column on the left and selecting Delete from the
context menu that appears.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                             Page 195 of 363
           Error status without interrupting the program is possible using Operator "MSG", see
           chapter 12.4.8.6.

MSG as independent test step:

The message belonging to the number behind MSG in the Nominal Value column of step 7 will be
written into the database together with registration data. The test will continue without interruption.

5       Capacity limit reached!
6       External control signal activated!

MSG as action:
You can also enter the MSG operator under the Action column.

If the program is in step 5 for 5:30 hours, an error output will occur. The message belonging to the
number entered behind MSG will be written into the database together with registration data.

           A single limit with MSG writes the message and goes to the next step. If there are two
           limits in the step and the MSG limit is reached first, the message is written and then it
           waits for the second limit to be reached.

           If there are two limits, it might happen that the step is left before the MSG limit is

Battery Manager User Manual                 99.3              Page 197 of 363
12.4.8.7 How to Use the "SAVE" Operator

This operator saves all battery data in the current registration format.

When a test is finished, the counts from channels that are assigned in the given registration format
are stored as a separate test section. You can now run any tests with this battery, without these
battery counts getting lost.

The SAVE operator stores the data into a test section whose name has to be entered in the
Nominal Value column. Several test sections for one battery are possible.

You can restore the counts for further tests with this battery by setting the REST operator. For
further explanations please see chapter 12.2.8.8.

12.4.8.8 How to Use the "REST" Operator

This operator restores previously saved data. This function allows you to continue a test at a later
date with all counts (Ah, Wh, etc.).

When you start this test, the counts previously stored by the SAVE command will be restored.
Therefore, with REST the name of the appropriate test section has to be given in the Nominal
Value column. The restoration only works for channels that have been saved with the same name
as used in the registration format that has to be declared before REST. In above example, this
registration format is STANDARD.

           A further condition for the function of the REST operator is the existence of the
           corresponding specific battery data stored with SAVE. Since the saved data is stored in
           the database, it must be available at the time of REST execution. If this is not the case,
           the running program waits or is interrupted.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                         Page 198 of 363
12.4.8.9 How to Use the "TABLE" Operator

A: Creating a Nominal Value Table with Notepad
To use a nominal value table in program editor, enter operator TABLE in column Operator and
enter the table's name in column Nominal Value.

To create a nominal value table you require a text editor, e.g. Notepad (started by clicking Start-
(All) Programs-Accessories-Notepad). You can define a nominal value table line by line using
the following pattern:

X;Y;Z;U;
with
X = duration in seconds (using sec behind value X), minutes (min) or hours (h)
Y = current in A
Z = power in W
U = voltage in V

Every value has to be completed with a semicolon ; .

You do not need to define four values in each line. One, two or three values are also possible.

If no unit is defined for duration, unit "seconds" is set.

Example:
A single line
30;
induces a pause for 30 seconds.

Minimum step times depend on the utilization rate of the entire BTS-600 system. For a single
circuit you can work with step times of down to 0.1 seconds.

Current and/or wattage values for discharge steps must be entered with a negative sign.

As a result, if only time and voltage are defined, this line always counts as a charge step even if
the voltage value is negative.

Example:
2 min;;;-18;

is a charge step because neither current nor wattage are defined as negative.

The table could for example look like this:

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 199 of 363
Save the nominal value table as a text file with the extension .TXT in the directory
C:\Digatron\Battery Manager\Server\BTS-600\Table. By calling the file name you can use the
table for your programs. See the following paragraph for more details.

B: Loading a Nominal Value Table from the Hard-Disk of your PC

In step 4 of the above program a nominal value list named FTP2.TXT will be loaded and executed.

The nominal value list on the hard-disk must have the extension .TXT, as described under A: and
must be stored in directory C:\Digatron\Battery Manager\Server\BTS-600\Table.

The name of the nominal value table must not be longer than 8 characters.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 200 of 363
C: Entry of Factors for the Current and Power Values in a Table

If you wish to use a nominal value table in several tests with different current and power values,
you have to enter appropriate factors into the Nominal Value column:

Current: Factor_I
Power: Factor_P
Voltage: Factor_U

Step 4 uses the nominal value table with the name FTP2.TXT. Each value in the table for current
and power is multiplied in the above example with the factor "0.5".

The factors must be entered in the Nominal Value column under the table name. The order of entry
is at random.

You can define these registrations together with operator TABLE:

1. no entry under Registration: automatic registration with every step change
2. entering a time, e.g. 5 sec for registrations every 5 seconds

   For this type of registration and the registrations described next you can deactivate
   automatic registration for each step change with a prior program step
   SET RLevel = 3.0. For more on RLevel see chapter 12.4.1.
3. current, e.g. 1 A for registration with each current change of 1 Ampere or more.
4. Ah and Wh, e.g. 1 Ah for registration with each completed Ah
5. >> time1 & time2, e.g. >> 1 min & 5 sec for registration after a minute and then every
   5 seconds

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 201 of 363
D: FUDS Test

By using the TABLE operator it is now possible to run the FUDS test (Federal Urban Driving
Schedule) which is an automobile industry standard vehicle time-velocity profile for urban driving
that has been used for a number of years for electric vehicle performance testing. Furthermore a
table for the Dynamic Stress Test (DST) and a table for the simplified FUDS test are implemented.
The tables for the FUDS and DST tests contain relative values of the peak power. The table for the
SFUDS contains values rated to W/kg. The following description gives an example how to use the
FUDS test.

The FUDS test is based on discharging the battery with a given power. The program must include
the TASK OUW for calculating the actual power which is taken from or given to the battery. The
channel name and the units name is Watt. Thus the actual power can be used in a registration
format by using Watt in a certain format.

The table used for the FUDS test is given in relative values. Thus a factor has to be added to each
relative value to get the absolute value of the Peak Power used for each step of the FUDS test.
This is done by using the Factor_P function. The value standing before this function is the used
Peak Power. In the example program above it is 100 Watt.

The FUDS test is normally terminated by a certain amount of ampere-hours removed from the
battery or if the battery reaches a certain voltage level. The example above terminates either if the
battery voltage is below 11.3 V or 50 Ah has been removed from the battery. If none of these
terminations are valid during a complete FUDS test that contains 1372 steps, the FUDS test starts
again at the beginning of the table.

Using the DST or SFUDS is equal to FUDS test however the table of the SFUDS is based on
W/kg. Thus the weight of active material has to be used as the factor for the SFUDS table and not
the absolute value for the peak power.

The names of the tables that are available and stored in the directory C:\Digatron\Battery
Manager\Server\BTS-600\Table are as follows:

fudstbl for the FUDS test
sfudstabl for the SFUDS test
dsttbl for the DST

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 202 of 363
E: Limitation of Table Values

Values in a table can be limited by using one or several of the following parameters together with a
TABLE step: Ap, An, Vp, Vn, Wp, Wn.

Ap = maximum allowed positive (charge) current in Ampere (in example 150 A)
An = maximum allowed negative (discharge) current in Ampere

         (in example 210 A  minimum value is limited to -210 A, entered as a positive value)
Vp = maximum allowed (charge) voltage in Volt (in example 60 V)
Vn = minimum allowed (discharge) voltage in Volt

         (in example 40 V, minus required for negative voltages)
Wp = maximum allowed positive (charge) power in Watt (in example 4000 W)
Wn = maximum allowed negative (discharge) power in Watt

         (in example 6500 W  minimum value is limited to -6500 W, entered as a positive value)

If values are not specified, the values specified inside the table or the maximum / minimum
physical circuit data will be used.

Instead of Profile you enter the name of the table you want to use.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 204 of 363
12.4.8.13 How to Use the "RANGE" Operator

Regarding the range switch there is a distinction between automatic and manual range switch.

Automatic range switch
After every program start the automatic range switch is enabled. With every new step, the system
automatically selects the correct range based on the actual set point for the current. If no set point
is given, range 1 will be selected, otherwise the system chooses the range with the lowest possible
boundary.
Manual range switch
As soon as the RANGE operator is set together with nominal values 1, 2, 3, or 4, (only 1 or 2 for
IGBT systems), the manual range switch is activated and remains valid for all following program
steps. To switch back to the automatic range switching you enter the RANGE operator with the
nominal value 0. Starting with this program step the automatic range switch is activated. Especially
with programs that contain many cycles and jumps you should check carefully whether the
intended range remains active.

Fast slew rates and transit times
The following examples a range switch for 50/200 Ampere has been realized.

To achieve fast slew rates and transit times, don't use the automatic range switch because during
the switching process the whole routine for current decrease and relay run-time is processed. If the
higher range is chosen (200 A) it remains defined for all subsequent steps, even if a nominal value
step with I < 50 A (range 1) is set.

Example 1: Discharge and charge within 200 A range

This program example realizes a fast slew rate from 10 A to 200 A. However, the charge would be
performed with the exactness of the higher range parameters. To change this, switching to the
range 1 before charging.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 205 of 363
Example 2: Discharge with 200 A range and charge with 50 A Range
Example 3: Optimum range for every step

In example 3, the best range for every step is set. Yet the optimum slew rate is not achieved.
Between the discharge with 10 A and the discharge with 200 A lies a transit time of 50ms. During
this pause the current is decreased to 0 A.

The IRANGE operator can be placed anywhere in the program. If that means a real change of the
range, then the actual physical range switch will be done inside the device control board at the
begin of the next step.

           There is no auto range switch during a step. With ramps and power values as nominal
           values as well as when using operator TABLE, there is no auto range switch either.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                 Page 206 of 363
12.4.8.14 How to Use the "PARALLEL" Operator

For MBT circuits:
Parallel mode is only possible for currents >50 A and separate charging power units. Only circuits
on the same MBT CPUs can be parallel. A maximum number of 7 circuits can be arranged in
parallel mode on one (master) circuit.

For ME circuits:
Parallel mode is only possible for circuits with a common A maximum number of 15 (slave) circuits
can be arranged in parallel mode on one (master) circuit.
Parallel mode is not possible for TEFEU circuits.

The only precondition for programming a parallel connection is the interconnection of two or more
circuits from one unit or one CPU. This linking is maintained by parallel connection of power and
sense connectors from the respective circuits.

The parallel connection of two or more circuits is defined by the BTS program exclusively. It is
initiated through the PARALLEL operator and a statement of the circuits involved in the Nominal
Value column. In the moment of the start these circuits have to be in STOP mode. If that is the
case, the circuits are being reserved for parallel operation and they are not available for individual
tests until the program is stopped.

For the following examples we assume that the circuits 4, 5 and 6 operate in a cabinet on the same
CPU.

Example 1: Parallel connection with circuit 6

Example 1 shows the programming of a parallel connection with circuit 6. If circuit 4 is now started
with this program and circuit 6 is in STOP mode, circuits 4 and 6 run in parallel mode. Thereby the
respective nominal value is divided according to the number of circuits involved. The readjustment
by the software only affects the currently started circuit.

If additional channels are not in STOP mode or do not share the same MBT CPU, an error
message appears and the current circuit (4) switches into interrupt mode. In case a program runs
on the parallel circuit at this time, it can be stopped and circuit 4 can be continued. Should the
wrong circuit address be chosen for the parallel circuit, the program on circuit 4 must be stopped,
modified and then be restarted.

Though multiple usage of the PARALLEL operator is possible, it wouldn't make much sense
because the connection of the battery would have to be changed during the course of the program.

           If you want to use a Safetask and operator PARALLEL, operator PARALLEL must
           be defined first before using operator TASK in the next program step.

While two or more circuits operate in parallel mode, they cannot be started together with other
programs. When trying to start a circuit that is currently in parallel mode, an error message
appears.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 207 of 363
When the program is terminated by the master (Example 1, circuit 4) during the normal program
course, circuit 4 remains in STOP mode and stays active in the dispo. Only by means of a manual
stop of the circuit the program is stopped completely and parallel mode is turned off. Only then the
circuits can again be used for individual tests.

The following example shows the parallel connection of 3 circuits:

Example 2: Parallel connection of 3 circuits

The parallel connection of more than two circuits is realized by extending the line with the
PARALLEL operator. If the above program is started on circuit 4, the circuits 4, 5 and 6 are run in
parallel mode.

Possible errors during parallel connection of circuits

First case:
A program is started with the PARALLEL operator and the error message "Current below set
value!" appears.

Possible Reason:
The circuit outputs are not connected. The master reports a fault because the preset current value
is not reached due to the current division.

Solution:
Connect the intended parallel circuit to the same battery and continue the master circuit.

Second case:
The intended current value cannot be reached though the circuits are properly parallel connected.

Possible Reasons:
You have chosen a current that lies above the possible maximum current that is the sum of the
circuits' maximum currents.

or
A current was chosen that exceeds the possible maximum current that results as the total of the
circuits' maximum currents for the chosen range.

Solution:
Examine the program, make appropriate changes and restart the master.

Third case:
After starting parallel mode, an error message appears.

Possible reasons:
You have chosen a parallel circuit that is outside of the cabinet of the master circuit.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 208 of 363
or
The chosen parallel circuit is currently running on another program.

Solution:
Change the program by choosing a parallel circuit within the same cabinet. Make sure that the
parallel circuit is really connected to the same battery as the master and restart the program.

or
Stop the parallel circuit. Make sure that the parallel circuit is really connected to the same battery
as the master and restart the program at the master.

Fourth case:
You simply want to use a circuit on its own. You start a program on the circuit an error message
appears.

Possible reasons:
The circuit is used as a parallel circuit in a test by another program.

or
The parallel mode might already be finished but the master has not yet been manually stopped.

Solution:
Choose another circuit to start your test. Make sure the battery is connected to the appropriate
circuit.

or
Stop the master manually, make sure that the batteries are connected to the right circuits and
restart the program.

Additional advice

Due to technical reasons, error messages that refer to parallel mode appear only when the
PARALLEL operator is reached in the course of the program.

Before starting parallel mode, check the program and the connections between chosen circuits and
battery.

Place the PARALLEL operator in the first line of a program if possible. Eventual error messages
will then appear shortly after the start of the program.

Check the intended nominal values within the program steps. The maximum current is the total of
the maximum currents from the respective circuits.

When using the RANGE operator you should carefully check the following program steps and their
nominal values and compare them to the maximum possible current. See also chapter 12.4.8.133
How to Use the "RANGE" Operator.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 209 of 363
12.4.8.15 How to Use the "EIS" Operator

Use operator EIS to realize an EIS step for the EIS-Meter. The following program example shows
the available possibilities:

Step     Label  Operator     Nominal Value        Limit      Action Registration
1               SET                                                      EisReg
2               PAU           25.0 ADC                5 sec
3               EIS           4.2 VDC                30 min
                              10 mHz
2                             6.0 kHz
                              2.0 AAcMax
                              4.2 VAcMax
                              3.0 VAcMin

                STO

Possible nominal values for operator EIS:

ADC      Nominal value optional ­ load current in Ampere by the circuit like in a normal

         charge step with operator CHA.

         ADC > 0             => charge step

         ADC < 0             => discharge step

         ADC not defined     => pause step

         ADC = 0             => pause step

VDC      Nominal value optional ­ voltage limit like in a normal charge step. Defining VDC
         is only possible if current ADC is also defined; otherwise an error occurs.

mHz, Hz, kHz Defines frequency for each measurement.
                   If two values are defined, a spectrum is measured with the smaller value being the
                   start frequency and the larger value being the end frequency. 8 measurements per
                   decade will be made.
                   If only one value is defined, measurement is only conducted at this frequency.

AAcMax   Optional ­ Maximum AC measuring current allowed. If this value is not defined, it
         will be set to 2 A.

VAcMax   Optional ­ Maximum voltage. When exceeded, EIS-METER indicates error. If this
         value is not defined, it will be set to 20 V.

VAcMin   Optional ­ Minimum voltage. When below, EIS-METER indicates error. If this value
         is not defined, it will be set to -2 V.

mVideal  Optional - Amplitude of the voltage response coming from the battery.
         For linearity reasons it should range between 3 and 10 mV / cell. EIS-METER
         measures a frequency and adjusts the stimulatory current amplitude in a way that
         mVideal is met as well as possible.
         3 mV / cell is a good choice. If this value is not defined, it will be set to 10 mV.

Battery Manager User Manual                 99.3             Page 210 of 363
EIStime   Optional ­ Minimum time for measurement per frequency.
          A frequency is measured for the time given with EIS time, but at least for 3 periods.
EISperi   With e.g. 1 Hz a measurement takes 3 seconds even when EIStime is defined as
AACstart  1 s. If this value is not defined, it will be set to 10 s.

          Optional ­ defines the number of periods per frequency. If not defined, value is
          internally set to 3.

          Optional ­ Current amplitude to start a spectrum.
          For safety reasons this parameter should initially be chosen small (e.g. 20 mA).
          However, EIS-METER might measure the frequency again with a larger amplitude
          due to a small voltage response. If you know your test object and you know what
          kind of impedance to expect at the beginning of the spectrum, you can choose a
          larger amplitude (e.g. 1 A). You thereby save the effort of a second measurement
          for the first frequency. If this value is not defined, it will be set to 20 mA.

Define limit for operator EIS:

n min     Defines a time limit. The user must set this time e.g. to protect the connected test
          object. The limit must be greater than the duration of the measurement.

Battery Manager User Manual     99.3  Page 211 of 363
12.4.8.16 How to Use the Operators "SYNCLine" and "SYNCProgram"

           The operators SYNCLine and SYNCProgram can be used for ME- and MBC systems.
           The old operator SYNC does no longer exist.
The operators SYNCLine and SYNCProgram are used to synchronize running programs on
circuits. Only circuits that have the same value entered in column SyncGroup of the Circuit View
are actually synchronized:

Usage
The use of SYNCLine and SYNCProgram is simple: the operator is inserted into the program as
an independent program step without further parameters.

Functionality
All running programs of the circuits of a SyncGroup are stopped in the step SYNCLine or
SYNCProgram until all associated programs are in this step SYNCLine or SYNCProgram. The
circuits are thus interrupted until all programs have reached this step. Subsequently, these circuits
are continued simultaneously with the following program step. The circuits have thus been
synchronized.

Difference between SYNCProgram and SYNCLine
With the SYNCProgram operator, synchronization only occurs for circuits in which the same
program with the same program version is running. SYNCLine does not pay attention to the
program name or program version.

12.5 Helpful Program Elements

Chapter 12.4.7 should give you a first impression on how to create a program. BTS-600 contains a
program language that can realize also complex tasks in a simple and easy-to-survey manner. The
following elements should assist you in creating and structuring complex programs.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 212 of 363
12.5.1 Cycles

Cycles are essential program elements. You can use them whenever you want to repeat the same
steps. A brief description of the necessary operators, namely BEG and CYC is given in chapter
12.4.2.

Programming a cycle is most simple:

Set the beginning of the cycle by entering the BEG operator in the Operator column. Enter a name
for the cycle in column Nominal Value. This is necessary if you will have to preset the counter of
this cycle with a certain value.

The cycle end is defined by the CYC operator. Define the number of cycles by entering a figure
and an asterisk " * " (e.g. 10*) in the Nominal Value column.

It is, of course, also possible to interlace up to 16 different cycles.

The following example shows two interlaced cycles:

The inner cycle (steps 4 thru 9) contains a two-step charge process, followed by a discharge step.
After the 5th repetition follows a 12-hour pause. Then the outer cycle (steps 3 thru 11) will start
from the beginning. The inner cycle is executed 5*10 = 50 times.

You can also use variables (defined with SET, see chapter 12.4.8.1) for the number of cycle runs:

The variable xyz was assigned the value 10 in step 1. In step 5 this variable is now used for the
number of cycles. The section between BEG and ZYK is thus repeated 10 times.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 213 of 363
12.5.2 Procedures

BTS-600 allows you to combine several test steps to one command - to create a procedure.
If you want the same sequence of test steps to be executed in different programs, it is useful to
combine these steps to a procedure. Procedures can be used in any program. Furthermore, they
make your programs easier to survey.
How to Create a Procedure
Procedures are created in the program editor. For example: a three-step charge process should be
combined to a procedure. On the one hand, the reduced number of test steps will make your
program clearer. On the other hand, you can use this procedure in any other program used for the
same battery type. Another positive aspect is that procedures reduce the error frequency in
program creation.
The original charge process comprises test steps 3 thru 5:

First select all lines that should be contained in the procedure. Lines are selected by dragging the
mouse along the Step column.
The selected program lines are now shown inverse.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 214 of 363
Then perform a right-click in the Step column of a highlighted column. Select Create procedure
from the appearing menu.

You are then prompted to enter a name and a free program number for the storage of the
procedure.

After clicking you have the possibility to enter a program number:

When clicking Next available Program Number a free number is chosen by the program itself.

After saving the procedure it can be found in the overview of all programs under BTS-600 in the
section Procedures.

You may also create a procedure by right-clicking the entry Procedures in the list of all BTS-600
programs as shown above and then click New Procedure from the appearing menu. After entering
a new name and clicking the Edit Program button you can create procedure steps in the program
editor.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                   Page 215 of 363
Once a procedure has been created, it can be used by entering its name in the Operator column
of a program.

Procedures can be edited and managed like programs.

When you display a running program and this program is currently in a procedure step, the

procedure is displayed in detail. Use button Show main program  in the right

upper part of the window to switch to the program. When back in the main program the button

changes and can be used to go back to the procedure again.

Decompose Procedures

To display single procedure steps, highlight the line that contains the procedure. A line can be
highlighted by clicking into the Step column. Now right-click in column Step and select Procedure
decomposition from the appearing menu. Procedure steps are now displayed inverse.

To recompose procedure steps, all procedure steps have to be highlighted. Then right-click in the
Step column of one of these steps and select Procedure recomposition from the appearing
menu.

           As with programs, also with procedures the name and the title should clearly show for
           which type of battery the procedure was created, respectively for which type of battery it
           is suited. Using the wrong procedures may have fatal effects on your battery!

Instead of the original test steps, the corresponding columns in a program now merely contain the
names of the selected procedure. The following test steps are renumbered automatically.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                               Page 216 of 363
Display Procedures
Procedures can be found in the overview of all programs under BTS-600 in the section
Procedures.

By double-clicking a procedure name the procedure is displayed in the program editor. As you are
in the editor window, you can edit the procedure like a normal program.

           Please note that, when you have modified a procedure, all other programs using
           this procedure will also be affected. In this case, you better decompose this procedure,
           then modify it and create a new procedure under a new name!

12.5.3 Counter Settings, GOTO Commands

Counter Setting
During testing operation, you will often have to interrupt tests and continue them afterwards in a
slightly modified form. When a test is stopped - i.e. terminated completely - the program must be
re-compiled before the test is continued. This will, of course, also reset all counters of the
preceding tests, i.e. all cycle counters will start with 1, all other counters (Ah, Wh, etc.) will start
with 0. Many programs use counts as limits. Therefore, write down the counts prior to the program
stop and redefine these counts in the program before continuing the test.
Enter a jump command to the point where the program was aborted to continue the test at the very
point.
Use the SET operator to set the counters and use GOTO operator to enter jump commands.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 217 of 363
The first step is to define the cycle counter and the Ah and Wh counters with the values of the last
registration before the program was aborted. To preset the cycle counter, first enter a cycle name.
Enter any name in the Nominal Value column at the beginning of the cycle (BEG operator) - in our
case "Cycle". It goes without saying that you can also preset the counters with values for several or
for interlaced cycles. Please make sure that the Ah and Wh counters show negative values! If
you forget to enter a minus sign, the battery will be discharged from 53.21 Ah to -80 Ah, i.e. by
another 133.21 Ah! Please see the entries in steps 1, 6 or 7 of above example.

Jumps
After a 30-second pause, the program will execute the jump to the abort point, marked with "Break"
above. If the cycle counter was not programmed with a SET command, the count will start with 1.
In our example the counter will start with 27.

The program will be processed as usual. Unless the cycle counter shows 1000, the two discharge
steps will be repeated until the limit of -80 Ah is reached. The GOTO command effects that the
program quits the cycle and goes to label "End".

The following brief example shows the most important features of the GOTO operator.

You can enter a jump command either as Operator or as Action. When used as operator, the
jump destination should be entered in the Nominal Value column, while with actions jump
destinations should be entered in the Action column. In both cases, the jump destination must be
clearly defined in the Label column. With a jump command, you can also enter a cycle or quit it.

The only restriction is that you cannot use a jump command to go to a sub-program (procedure) or
to return from a sub-program to the main program. The jump destination must always be on the
same program level!

To realize conditional branches, use the GOTO command in the Action column.

Depending on which limit is reached, the test will branch at four different program points: Either to
the next program step after 30 seconds or, before that, to one of the three labels "End", "Charge"
or "Error".

This permits a dynamic organization of the test process, based on battery parameters.

           The following label names are not allowed: V, A, mA, Ah, mAh, AhCha, AhLad, mAhCha,
           mAhLad, AhDch, AhEla, mAhDch, mAhEla, AhStep, AhPas, mAhStep, mAhPas, AhPrev,
           AhPrec, mAhPrev, mAhPrec, AhStat, mAhStat, AhBal, mAhBal, Wh, mWh, WhCha,
           WhLad, mWhCha, mWhLad, WhDch, WhEla, mWhDch; mWhEla, WhStep, WhPas,
           mWhStep, mWhPas, WhPrev, WhPrec, mWhPrev, mWhPrec.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 218 of 363
12.5.4 Special Labels

ONERROR:
The entry ONERROR is made in the column Operator. The name of the label is to be entered into
the nominal value column. In the case of an error, the program jumps to the step with that label
name.
The program part provided for the case of an error should be placed at the end of the program, as
all steps to the operator STO will be executed.
Example:

Due to the ONERROR function in step 6, the battery will be discharged in the case of an error for a
time of 10 minutes with 20 A (step 10).

           After the jump initiated by ONERROR you need to implement a CLEAR command first
           (step 9). Please also refer to the explanations about CLEAR given in chapter
           12.4.8.1010.

ONEXIT:
The entry ONEXIT is made in the column Operator. The name of the label is to be entered into the
nominal value column. The steps under the label will be executed after the end of the program.
Example:

The ONEXIT function in step 6 in this example ensures that a message is transmitted to the host
computer after the end of the program.
