Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                 Page 221 of 363
13.2.1 Operator "SYN"

           The operator SYN is only available for BTS-550 programs.

If several circuits run with the same program and program version, each circuit remains in step
SYN until all circuits with this program are in this (same) SYN step. Then all circuits are
simultaneously and automatically continued. Circuits are thus synchronized.

           SYN must not be the first step in a program.

           Before each SYN step another step of at least 1 minute must be inserted, e.g. Charge
           (CHA) or Pause (PAU). Between two SYN steps in the same program there needs to be
           a step of at least 1 minute as well, e.g. PAU.
Program example:

The operator SYN is used in step 6. All circuits with this program are interrupted when step 6 is
reached. After all circuits reached step 6, the circuits are simultaneously and automatically
continued.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 222 of 363
13.3 Nominal Values

The nominal values determine the general conditions for the different program steps. The entry in
the program column Nominal Value is made in the following form:
<figure> <unit>, e.g. 500 A or 144 V.
Each entry of nominal value must always be made with value and unit. A different form of entry is
only allowed if a ramp function is programmed. In this case, the entry must be made as follows: 5 ­
200 A for an increasing current or 100 ­ 2 A for an decreasing current. See following program
example.

                       Example: Program lines for a ramp function

If several nominal values for one program step are entered one below the other, the control in the
power unit will ensure that none of the values is exceeded. The following example shows a typical
IU charge.

                       Example: Program lines for an IU charge

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 223 of 363
13.4 Limits

Limits determine the moment for the transition to the next program step. In principle, the following
entries are permitted in the program column Limit (also see chapter 13.1 Program Parameters):

Voltage:      Values in V with one digit after the decimal point        1)
Current:
Temperature:  Values in A with one digit after the decimal point        1)

Capacity:     Values in degrees Celsius with one digit after
Energy:
Time:         the decimal point: e. g. 98.5 C or - 30.0 C               2)

              Values in Ah with one digit after the decimal point       1)

              Values in kWh with one digit after the decimal point      1)

              Values in hours : minutes : seconds up to maximum

              "999:59:59h", "32767:59h" or "32767h". The time may

              as well be entered in the form of "30 sec" or "0.5 min".

1)            The number of possible digits after the decimal point

              depends on the technical data of the power unit.

2)            Only if equipped with the respective option!

It is also allowed to set the sign ">" (= greater than) or "<" (= smaller than) as a prefix to the limit.
If no prefix is entered, ">" (greater than) will always be assumed for program steps with the
operators CHA and RCH, and "<" (smaller than) for program steps with the operators DCH and
PAU. If several limits for one program step are entered one below the other, the one reached first
will trigger the transition to the next program step.

Please note that during the charge steps (CHA) and recharge steps (RCH) the capacity and
energy values are added and during discharge steps (DCH) they are subtracted. Therefore, the
sign must be observed for the entry of capacity and energy values.

If a value in Ah or kWh is entered as a limit, please be aware of the fact that during the entire
program process these values will be added in "charge steps" and subtracted in "discharge steps".
Please take account of this "balancing" when entering the limit values. The capacity and energy
values defined with the SET operator will also be taken into account at the respective point of the
program. Therefore, you have to make sure that any limit value entered in Ah or kWh will not yet
be reached in the preceding program step, because otherwise the program step with that limit
value would be skipped.

The automatic adding of the Ah and kWh counter values at the beginning of each program step
can be suppressed. For a reset of the counters after each program step you have to change the
DIL switch setting. For further details see "DIL switch setting: 64" in the calibration instructions.

13.4.1 Number of Cycles, Cycle Limit Values

A cycle can only be terminated by the entry of the number of cycles with "*" in the Nominal Value
column or by means of a step skip.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                           Page 224 of 363
13.4.2 Fixed Step Times and Program Operation Times

By means of the switch sign "/" it is possible to program fixed step times or fixed program operation
times.
To program a fixed step time enter the "numerical value/time unit" as limit value after the operator
PAU (= pause), e.g. 3 /h. As time units also min or sec are permitted.

                                              Example 1

The entry 3.0 /h in the second line limits the time for the first two program steps to maximum 3 h.
With the entries in program lines 3 and 4 the battery is charged to a limit value of 200 Ah according
to IU characteristics. When the capacity of 200 Ah is reached, there will be a pause until the total
time is 5 hours.

           Make sure that the fixed step time "x /h" is long enough to ensure that the limit condition
           of the previous program step is reached within this step time.
Further to fixed step times, it is also possible to enter fixed program operation times. This mode of
operation can be programmed with the SET operator and the limit value "0 /h". See line 2 in the
following example. The program time meter is set back to zero and the time of the following
program steps will be counted until a limit value entered as "x /h" is reached that deactivates this
mode of operation. See second last line in the following example.

                                              Example 2

The above example shows:

If an operation time of 5 hours is not exceeded in the program steps 3 and 4, the second last step
will be continued until the limit value of "5 /h" is reached.
The three program steps with the CHA operator are in this case limited to a total time of 5 hours.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 225 of 363
13.4.3 Charge Factor

If certain current quantities are discharged from fully charged batteries, the current quantities
required for a subsequent full charge will be higher. The relation of the current quantity required for
the full charge to the current quantity discharged before (= charge factor) can be taken into
account in program steps with the CHA operator.

                                            Example 1

At the start of each program step (except pause steps) the counts of the Ah counter and of the
kWh counter are automatically set back to the start values "0 Ah" and "0 kWh", if the DIL switch
"64" is set accordingly.
In the above example, the Ah counter has counted -10 Ah before the start of the second pause
step. If a charge factor of "1.1" shall apply for the subsequent charge step, this must be
programmed by entering the limit value "110 %Ah". In this case the charged current quantity
refers to the current quantity discharged in the last discharge step before.
A programmed charge factor may also apply to several charge steps if the SET operator with the
limit value "0 %Ah" has been entered before. This entry will set the Ah counter to 0 Ah and the
current quantities will be added till the next limit value entered as "%Ah".

                                              Example 2

In the above example, the Ah counter shows a value of -400 Ah after the discharge step. Due to
the limit value of 110 %Ah (= charge factor 1.1) the battery will be charged with a total of 440 Ah.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 226 of 363
                                              Example 3

Example 3 shows a programming of cycles with the two program steps to be repeated ten times. If
the charge factor is matched for the battery to be tested, the latter will have the same charge level
at the begin of each cycle. Therefore, the cycle times will be constant, although no discharge time
has been entered.

                                              Example 4

Example 4 shows a cyclic repetition of one discharge step followed by charge steps. After the
discharge the Ah counter shows a value of -400 Ah. The limit values 0.0 h and 0.0 %Ah after the
SET operator determine the start conditions for the following charge steps: time counter and Ah
counter are reset to zero. The time of the first charge step until the limit value of 148 Volts is
unknown. This also goes for the second charge step. The latter will only be terminated when the
charge current is less than 5 A or when the charged current quantity in both charge steps has
reached 440 Ah (= charge factor 1.1). The time of the third charge step is automatically limited to
the effect that the total charge time is 5 hours and the time of a cycle 7 hours.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 227 of 363
13.5 Abort Values

If one of the values entered into the program column Abort is reached, the program operation will
be interrupted immediately. It is distinguished between exceeding (marked by ">") and falling
below (marked by "<").

The program operation may be continued after an interruption. This can be done by a new start
command at the host computer or by pressing the start button at the formation circuit. If an abort
value is reached, this will be indicated by a message on the screen stating the respective circuit
address.

Abort values should always be entered if undesired battery conditions are likely to be expected. In
principle, you may enter into the program column Abort the same values as those allowed as limits
mentioned in chapter 13.4.

13.6 Registration

Values entered into the program column Registration determine the conditions under which or the
time when the measured values will be registered. If no entry is made in this column, the
measured values will only be registered at the beginning and at the end of each program step.
This will be the same for program steps within cycles. In principle, the same entries as for the limits
are allowed as registration conditions.

The most common entry is normally a time value. A time entered as registration condition means a
time interval after which a registration will be created.

If time intervals are entered for the registration you should observe the length of the respective
program step. If you choose your registration conditions appropriately you will get clear and
informative registration data, avoiding the printout of endless lists of measured data.

An entered time value may also be defined as an absolute value if it is preceded by an equal
sign "=". In this case, an additional registration will be made when the time entered has elapsed
since the start of the program step. If several time values are entered for the same program step
with or without the equal sign "=", the entered time interval will be fixed again when the absolute
value has been reached. See "Example for registration with absolute value" on the next page.

By means of the SET operator, you may enter random start values for the following program step
for the amphour counter and the watt-hour counter. It is as well possible to take over the counting
from the preceding program step, just by entering the SET operator before the new program step.

           Example for a program line with "SET" registration condition

In the above example the counter is set to a value of 440 Ah by means of the SET operator, thus
fixing the start value of the internal amphour counter for the following program step.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 228 of 363
Example for a program line with "SET" operator

In this example the SET operator is used to take over the counter values from the preceding step
as start values for the next following charge step.

                       Example for registration with absolute value

Registration in the second program step:

1. Registration at the start of the step
2. Registration after 2 minutes
3. Registration after 180 seconds
4. Registration after 5 minutes (= 180 sec + 2 min)
5. Registration after 7 minutes
etc.

13.6.1 Extended Registration Conditions

General Description:

The software for the formation system MicroForm 2000 has been extended. As from the software
version VC10.001.010 (delivery from March 1996) it is possible to program three different degrees
of registration:

· Degree 0: no registration        Entry: 0 Reg

· Degree 1: registration at the end of Entry: 1 Reg

a program step or a cycle

· Degree 2: standard registration  Entry: 2 Reg

Battery Manager User Manual        99.3              Page 229 of 363
1. Degrees of registration for the whole program
The entry is made with the SET operator in the program column Registration.

The respective entry is valid until a new degree of registration is entered with the SET operator.
2. Degree of registration for a single program step
The entry is made in the program column Registration of the respective program step.

The respective entry is only valid for the respective step and it has priority over the entry as per
section 1. It is thus possible to program random program steps with their own degrees of
registration.
3. Examples for Registration Conditions

No registration is made in this step, due to the fact that the entry "0 Reg" invalidates the
registration condition "1 sec".

No registration is made in the first step. In the second step a registration is made at the beginning
and at the end of the program step and during the step in intervals of one second.

No registration is made in the third step due to the fact that the entry "0 Reg" in the first step is also
valid for the third step.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 230 of 363
After the BEG operator only the entry "1 Reg" has a function. This is also valid for the CYC
operator.

With these two entries it is possible to have only the registration values at the beginning and at the
end of a cycle registered, under the condition that "0 Reg" has been entered with the SET operator
before the beginning of the cycle.

13.7 Program Examples

13.7.1 Input of Formation Programs

When a formation program is created, the system will automatically add a program step number to
each line that begins with an operator. This program step number is automatically corrected if lines
are added or deleted. The program step numbers will also appear on the printouts.
In the case of formation programs with a length of several hundred steps this number will give you
direct information on the present position within the formation program.

13.7.1.1 Examples of Formation Programs

Program step numbers in a formation program

Battery Manager User Manual                  99.3  Page 231 of 363
Interlaced cycles

Explanation:
The first cycle begins with program step number 11, ends with step number 20 and shall be
executed two times. The second cycle begins with formation step number 14, ends with formation
step number 17 and shall be executed three times. The second cycle will be executed six times in
total, as it is part of the first cycle.

13.7.2 Step Skipping in a Formation Program

The different program steps are, as explained earlier, numbered and thus defined. Any general
limit value may be used to trigger the skipping to another step. Enter the target step number
directly after the entry of the respective limit value or nominal value, leaving a blank between the
value and the step number.

           The entry of the target step number must always be separated by a blank, as otherwise it
           will not be possible for the program compiler to identify it.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 232 of 363
13.7.2.1 Admissible Step Skips

In general, skipping is allowed to any step in the program that has been assigned with a step
number, i.e. also to those steps that do not involve a direct action, such as BEG or SET. Skipping
out of a cycle or into a cycle is also possible, however, under respect of certain conditions. See
chapter 3.5 of the MF-2000 manual.
A target step number may be added to the following limit values or conditions:

 · Current values in amperes (A)
 · Voltage values in volts (V)
 · Temperature values in degrees Celsius (C)
 · Capacity values in ampere-hours (Ah)
 · Energy values in watt-hours (Wh)
 · Capacity values with charge factor (%Ah)
 · Fixed program run times in hours (/h)
 · Number of cycles ("*")
 · Limits for additional channels, e.g. 1.7V1

13.7.2.2 Syntax

The entry of limit values to limit a step is explained in chapter 13.4. To include a step skip in the
program, add the desired target step number to the limit value, leaving a blank between the limit
value and the step number. If a step shall be terminated for example as soon as the voltage is
higher than 14.4 Volts, you have to enter 14.4 V into the Limit column. If, however, you wish to
skip to another but the following step, e.g. step number 15, when this voltage is reached, you have
to enter 14.4 V 15. The next chapter shows some examples for the input.

13.7.2.3 Input Examples

etc.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 233 of 363
Program Example for the Skip Function (GOTO)

If the voltage falls below 11 Volts before the elapse of one hour, the program will be continued with
step number 14, otherwise it will be continued with the next following step.

After elapse of one hour, the program will be continued with step number 15.

If the step time reaches 15 hours before 130 %Ah of the preceding discharge step are reached,
step 16 will be terminated and the program continued with step 18.

When all 150 cycles were executed successfully, the program will start again with step 1.

13.7.2.4 Special Features of the Skip Function

If a cycle is discontinued due to a step skip, the present value of the cycle counter will be
memorized, and counting will be continued when the program goes back into this cycle.
If a cycle is discontinued before the CYC step is reached, the cycle counter will not continue
counting.
If the regular program run reaches a step with BEG or if the program jumps to a step with BEG, the
respective cycle counter will be set to "1".

14.3 Nominal Values

The nominal values determine the general conditions for the different program steps. The entry in
the program column Nominal Value is made in the following form:
<figure> <unit>, e.g. 50 A or 14.4 V.

           In all program steps with CHA, RCH or DCH operators you always have to enter a
           nominal current, power or resistance! Never enter a voltage value only!

Each entry of nominal value must always be made with value and unit. A different form of entry is
only allowed if a ramp function is programmed. In this case, the entry must be made as follows: 5 -
20 A for an increasing current or 10 - 2 A for a decreasing current. See following program
example.

                        Example: Program lines for a ramp function

If several nominal values for one program step are entered one below the other, the control in the
power unit will ensure that none of the values is exceeded. The following example shows a typical
IU charge.

14.4 Limits

The limits determine the moment for the transition to the next program step. In principle, the
following entries are permitted in the program column Limit (also see 14.1 Program Parameters):

Voltage:      Values in V with one digit after the decimal point        1)
Current:
Temperature:  Values in A with one digit after the decimal point        1)

Capacity:     Values in degrees Celsius with one digit after
Energy:
Time:         the decimal point: e. g. 98.5 C or - 30.0 C               2)

              Values in Ah with one digit after the decimal point       1)

              Values in Wh with one digit after the decimal point       1)

              Values in hours : minutes : seconds up to maximum

              "999:59:59h", "32767:59h" or "32767h". The time may

              as well be entered in the form of "30 sec" or "0.5 min".

1)            The number of possible digits after the decimal point

              depends on the technical data of the power unit.

2)            Only if equipped with the respective option!

It is also allowed to set the sign ">" (= greater than) or "<" (= smaller than) as a prefix to the limit
value. If no prefix is entered, ">" (greater than) will always be assumed for program steps with the
operators CHA and RCH, and "<" (smaller than) for program steps with the operator DCH and
PAU. If several limits for one program step are entered one below the other, the one reached first
will trigger the transition to the next program step.

Please note that during the charge steps (CHA) and recharge steps (RCH) the capacity and
energy values are added and during discharge steps (DCH) they are subtracted. Therefore, the
sign must be observed for the entry of capacity and energy values.

If a value in Ah or Wh is entered as limit, the ampere-hour counters and watt-hour counters will
automatically be set back to the start values "0 Ah" and "0 Wh" at the begin of each program
step. This is not valid for steps with the operator PAU.

The automatic resetting of the Ah and Wh counter at the beginning of each program step can be
suppressed. For an accumulating counting over all program steps you have to change the DIL
switch setting. For further details see "DIL switch setting: 64" in the calibration instructions.

For an accumulating counting over certain program steps, you may add a line with the SET
operator. The present Ah count and Wh count will then be taken as start values for the next
following step. The program line with the SET operator should not contain any other entry. Also
see example in chapter 14.6 Registration.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                           Page 239 of 363

Fixed Step Times and Program Operation Times

By means of the switch sign "/" it is possible to program fixed step times or fixed program operation
times.
To program a fixed step time enter the "numerical value/time unit" as limit value after the operator
PAU (= pause), e.g. 3 /h. As time units also min or sec are permitted.

                                              Example 1

The entry 3.0 /h in the second line limits the time for the first two program steps to a maximum of
3 h.

With the entries in program lines 3 and 4 the battery is charged to a limit value of 10 Ah according
to IU characteristics. When the capacity of 10 Ah is reached, there will be a pause until the total
time is 5 hours.

Important:

Make sure that the fixed step time "x /h" is long enough to ensure that the limit condition of the
previous program step is reached within this step time. Further to fixed step times, it is also
possible to enter fixed program operation times. This mode of operation can be programmed with
the SET operator and the limit value "0 /h". See line 2 in the following example. The program time
meter is set back to zero and the time of the following program steps will be counted until a limit
value entered as "x /h" is reached that deactivates this mode of operation. See second last line in
the following example.

If an operation time of 5 hours is not exceeded in the program steps 3 and 4, the second last step
will be continued until the limit value of "5 /h" is reached.

The three program steps with the CHA operator are in this case limited to a total time of 5 hours.

Charge Factor

If certain current quantities are discharged from fully charged batteries, the current quantities
required for a subsequent full charge will be higher. The relation of the current quantity required for
the full charge to the current quantity discharged before (= charge factor) can be taken into
account in program steps with the CHA operator.

                                              Example 1

At the start of each program step (except pause steps) the counts of the Ah counter and of the Wh
counter are automatically set back to the start values "0 Ah" and "0 Wh".

In the above example, the Ah counter has counted -10 Ah before the start of the second pause
step. If a charge factor of "1.1" shall apply for the subsequent charge step, this must be
programmed by entering the limit value "110 %Ah". In this case the charged current quantity
refers to the current quantity discharged in the last discharge step before.

A programmed charge factor may also apply to several charge steps if the SET operator with the
limit value "0 %Ah" has been entered before. This entry will set the Ah counter to 0 Ah and the
current quantities will be added till the next limit value entered as "%Ah".

Battery Manager User Manual             99.3  Page 241 of 363
                                              Example 2

In the above example, the Ah counter shows a value of -40 Ah after the discharge step. Due to the
limit value of 110 %Ah (= charge factor 1.1) the battery will be charged with a total of 44 Ah.

                                              Example 3

Example 3 shows a programming of cycles with the two program steps to be repeated ten times. If
the charge factor is matched for the battery to be tested, the latter will have the same charge level
at the begin of each cycle. Therefore, the cycle times will be constant, although no discharge time
has been entered.

                                              Example 4

Example 4 shows a cyclic repetition of one discharge step followed by charge steps. After the
discharge the Ah counter shows a value of -40 Ah. The limit values 0.0 /h and 0.0 %Ah after the
SET operator determine the start conditions for the following charge steps: time counter and Ah
counter are reset to zero. The time of the first charge step until the limit value of 14.8 Volts is
unknown. This also goes for the second charge step. The latter will only be terminated when the
charge current is less than 5 A or when the charged current quantity in both charge steps has
reached 44 Ah (= charge factor 1.1). The time of the third charge step is automatically limited to
the effect that the total charge time is 5 hours and the time of a cycle 7 hours. The following
diagram shows one cycle.

14.5 Abort Values

If one of the values entered into the program column Abort is reached, the program operation will
be interrupted immediately. It is distinguished between exceeding (marked by ">") and falling
below (marked by "<").

The program operation may be continued after an interruption. This can be done by a new start
command at the host computer or by pressing the start button at the test circuit. If an abort value is
reached, this will be indicated by a message on the screen stating the respective circuit address.
Abort values should always be entered if undesired battery conditions are likely to be expected. In
principle, you may enter into the program column Abort the same values as those allowed as limits
mentioned in chapter 14.4 Limits.

14.6 Registration

Values entered into the program column Registration determine the conditions under which or the
time when the measured values will be registered. If no entry is made in this column, the
measured values will only be registered at the beginning and at the end of each program step.
This will be the same for program steps within cycles. In principle, the same entries as for the
limits are allowed as registration conditions.

The most common entry is normally a time value. A time entered as registration condition means a
time interval after which a registration will be created.

If time intervals are entered for the registration you should observe the length of the respective
program step. If you choose your registration conditions appropriately you will get clear and
informative registration data, avoiding the printout of endless lists of measured data.

An entered time value may also be defined as an absolute value if it is preceded by an equal sign
"=". In this case, an additional registration will be made when the time entered has elapsed since
the start of the program step. If several time values are entered for the same program step with or
without the equal sign "=", the entered time interval will be fixed again when the absolute value
has been reached. See Example for registration with absolute value below.

By means of the SET operator, you may enter random start values for the following program step
for the amphour counter and the watt-hour counter. It is as well possible to take over the counting
from the preceding program step, just by entering the SET operator before the new program step.

           Example for a program line with SET registration condition

In the above example the counter is set to a value of 44 Ah by means of the SET operator, thus
fixing the start value of the internal amphour counter for the following program step.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 243 of 363

15 PLT Programs

15.1 Operation and Test Process

15.1.1 Description of the Test Process

The Production Line Tester PLT is designed for the continuous quality control and final
inspection of batteries of different type and design.

The battery test consists of two successive automatic test steps: an initial test of the open circuit
voltage as well as of the battery temperature (control of acid density), followed by a high rate
discharge test. The latter test, however, being performed only, if the test criteria fixed for the first
test step had been met.

Configuration of the Test Fixture:
The PLT unit is always placed at the end of a production line, each production line being equipped
with a separate Production Line Tester, the necessary conveyor mechanism with test head as well
as a conveyor control unit SPS.

Each PLT is provided with a microcomputer interface, performing the test fully automatically in
compliance with a program stored in the interface, the parameters of the test process to be fixed by
the user.

At the end of the test, a comparison of the different test results within one test series is made on
the basis of statistically determined comparison data and the test result is rated as either
"accepted" or "rejected".

If a battery complies with all test criteria, it will be rated as "accepted", in the negative case, it will
be rated as "rejected". A battery that is rated as "rejected" can thus be directly sorted out and
diverted to a separate conveyor. The PLT gives the signal to move the points (= potential-free
relay contact).

The comparison data are calculated anew after each test on the basis of the parameters fixed in
the program. The program parameters are entered either at the terminal or at the host computer.

The terminal allows the storage of a maximum of 100 programs, whereas a maximum of 999
programs can be stored if operation is made via a host computer. Program selection is made
directly at the decade switches of the PLT and confirmed by means of a key switch. All programs
can be easily modified. The maximum number of tests per minute depends on the time for the high
rate discharge test fixed in the program for the high rated discharge test on the one hand and on
the conveyor mechanism on the other. If e.g. the time for the high rate discharge test is
5 seconds, it is possible to test approximately six to eight batteries per minute.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 245 of 363
15.1.1.1 Arrangement of the Test Fixture

Figure 1 shows the general arrangement of the components of a test fixture. At the end of the
battery production line, there is always a PLT. The PLT is a high rate discharge tester, particularly
designed for the requirements in the production. Each PLT is provided with an internal
microcomputer interface, ensuring the control of the test process in compliance with the selected
test program. The control for the arrival of the battery in the test fixture, its positioning under the
test head, the touch-down of the test head on the battery terminals and finally for pushing out the
battery at the end of the test is effected by the conveyor control unit.

The sequential coordination between the production line and the PLT is ensured through digital
"I/O boards" (relay and photocoupler) on the PLT and the conveyor control unit. At the end of the
test fixture there are points with a diversion conveyor. All rejected batteries are pushed on this
diversion conveyor. The PLT gives the control signals to the conveyor control unit for moving the
points and pushing the battery on the diversion conveyor.

If several Production Line Testers are operated simultaneously, it is possible to enter the
programs and control the results at a central terminal. In that case, you have to connect the
microcomputer interfaces of the different PLTs via a partyline either to the terminal or to a PC.

15.1.1.2 Start of a Test Program

After the program selection (setting of the program number at the decade switch and confirmation
with the key switch at the PLT), every test program can be started directly at the conveyor. The
programmable control unit will then perform the test of the batteries passing through the test
fixture, without any interference of the terminal or host computer.

All test results are fully automatically evaluated by the PLT. All the result data of the present test
series can be viewed at any time on the monitor of the terminal or host computer.

In the case of operation via terminal, it is only possible to view the result data in the display sector
"Measuring" and "Statistical Data", whereas if operated via a host computer, it is also possible
to view data at a later date for evaluation and to have them printed. Figure 1 shows an example for
the arrangement of the test fixture.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 246 of 363
Fig. 1: General Arrangement of the Test Fixture

15.1.2 Integration of the Test Fixture into the Production Process

The test fixture is controlled through the conveyor control unit of the production line. The conveyor
control is usually made with a stored program system (SPS). The synchronization between test
process and production process is made through digital inputs/outputs of the conveyor control unit
and the PLT.

The test proceeds in the following steps:

 · Arrival of the battery to be tested...
 · Start of test ...
 · Test and test decision ...
 · Moving of points and diversion of battery ...
 · Arrival of the next battery ...

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 247 of 363
15.1.2.1 Arrival of a Battery

The battery arrives on the conveyor in the test fixture and is automatically positioned under the test
head. Then the test head is placed on the battery terminals. Together with the test head, also the
temperature probe for measuring the temperature is moved to the right measuring position.

15.1.2.2 Start of Test

After the steps described under point 15.1.2.1 above, the conveyor control will give a START
signal to the PLT. The PLT recognizes the START signal and confirms it by emitting the signal
CONFIRM START thus switching off the internal start relay of the PLT. The position of the internal
PLT start relay can be enquired by the conveyor control, if required. With the signal CONFIRM
START the PLT assumes control over the further test process.

15.1.2.3 Test and Test Evaluation

Upon the start signal, the PLT will perform the test automatically. The test process is determined
by the test program stored in the PLT. The result data will then be calculated from the measuring
results of the test. A comparison between the result data and the comparison data will then result
in the test evaluation. The following three evaluations are possible:

Test Decision                Signification / Function
ACCEPT                       Battery "ACCEPTED"
REJECT                       Battery "REJECTED"  divert
                             Battery "REJECTED"  divert and abort further test
REJECT, ABORT                process

Batteries are rated as "accepted", if the test criteria are met, otherwise they are rated as
"rejected".

With the emission of either the result signal ACCEPT, REJECT or REJECT together with the
ABORT signal, the PLT passes the control again over to the conveyor control unit. Please note
that in the case of an abort of the test process, at first a REJECT signal is given, which is followed
by an ABORT signal.

15.1.2.4 Control of Pushing Out and Sorting Out

The signals "ACCEPT" and "REJECT" indicate to the conveyor control that the battery had been
tested. The conveyor control will then trigger the lifting of the test head and the battery will then be
pushed out. Batteries with the test result "REJECT" will be diverted to a separate conveyor.

15.1.2.5 Abort of the Test Process

If an "ABORT" signal is given, the test fixture will not be ready for a new test and the continuous
test process will be stopped. The abort signal may be used to release an alarm signal to the
operating personnel at the conveyor control (e. g. signal lamp or sound signal).

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 248 of 363
Upon elimination of the fault causing the abort and acknowledgement of the fault signal, it is
possible to start the test process again.

15.1.2.6 Abort Conditions

The test process will be aborted under the following conditions:

Abort due to abort criteria of the PLT

 Three "rejected" batteries in a row

 Unacceptable high current variation during high rate discharge test

 Final voltage after high rate discharge too low

 The tolerance range calculated after the last test for one of the measuring quantities exceeds
      the limit values entered in the program.

      If one of the limit values has been exceeded, the PLT will only be ready for testing the next
      battery after a new selection of program. The program selection will automatically start a new
      averaging.

15.1.2.7 Start Repetition

It may occasionally happen that after the first touch-down of the test head on the battery terminals,
the oxide film is not removed well enough to ensure a perfect conductive connection. In that case,
either no voltage is measured, or no test current is flowing. Such a battery would be rated as
"rejected", although this is only due to a contact failure.

In order to prevent such an obviously wrong evaluation, the test will automatically be repeated. The
PLT conveys the signal START REPETITION to the conveyor control. The test head will be lifted
and placed on the terminals again. Normally, there will be a sufficiently conductive connection
when the test head is placed on the terminals a second time.

The conveyor control will then again give a START signal to the PLT and the test process will be
continued. The signal for a start repetition is given only once. If there is still a contact failure, the
battery will be rated as "rejected" and an error message will be given.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 249 of 363
15.1.2.7.1 Conditions for a Start Repetition

A signal for a start repetition will be given under the following conditions:
 Current lower than 10 % of the nominal current: Iact < 0.1 Inom

 The value of the open circuit voltage is lower than 10 % of the maximum
      nominal battery voltage: | UOCV| < 10 % Unom

15.1.3 Test Process and Test Evaluation

Each battery is evaluated on the basis of two subsequent test steps, the second step (high rate
discharge test) being carried out only, if the first one was successful:
· Measuring of the battery temperature and open circuit voltage
· High rate discharge test
The evaluation is made at the end of both test steps.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                     Page 254 of 363
15.1.3.1 Measuring of Temperature and Open Circuit Voltage

The temperature measuring can e. g. be made with a radiation probe for non-contact measuring of
temperature or any other suitable probe.

A measuring amplifier provides a voltage in proportion to the temperature which will be converted
into the respective temperature value in °C.

Temperature and open circuit voltage are measured at the beginning of each measuring cycle. The
high rate discharge test is only carried out, if both the battery temperature and the open circuit
voltage are within the limits fixed in the program. The battery voltage is measured through separate
measuring contacts at the test head.

15.1.3.2 High Rate Discharge Test

The following test parameters may be fixed individually in the different programs for the high rate
discharge test:

I       = high rate discharge current
t       = high rate discharge time
Uabort  = abort voltage after high rate discharge

During the high rate discharge test, the battery is discharged for the time "t" with the high rate
discharge current "I". During that time, the battery voltage should not fall below the programmed
minimum voltage Uabort.

During the high rate discharge test, the battery voltage and the high rate discharge current are
continuously measured and checked versus the programmed limit values. At the end of the test
time, the voltage measured after the high rate discharge UHRD will be recorded for evaluation of
the results.

15.1.3.2.1 Failure during High Rate Discharge Test

The following failures may occur during the high rate discharge test:

· If during the high rate discharge test, the battery voltage falls below the programmed final
    voltage Uabort, the test will be terminated before the fixed time has elapsed and the battery
    will be rated as "rejected".

· If the high rate discharge current I differs by more than 1% from the nominal current of the PLT
      (e.g. by ± 15 A in the case of a PLT with a nominal current of 1500 A), the running test will be
      terminated and the battery rated as "rejected". In that case, the test process will be aborted
      and the ABORT signal given. Test result Current is displayed.

The high rate discharge voltage UHRD measured at the end of the test time will be recorded and
evaluated according to the defined limits.

Battery Manager User Manual        99.3            Page 255 of 363
15.1.3.3 Compensation of Temperature

If the difference between the battery temperature measured and the reference temperature fixed at
27 °C is not too great, it can normally be assumed that the open circuit voltage of the battery is a
linear function of its temperature.

From the battery temperature measured at the beginning of the test and the open circuit voltage
you can thus calculate the open circuit voltage UOCV of the battery referred to a reference
temperature of 27 °C. A special menu for system setting allows you to adapt the correction factor
individually to the type of battery to be tested.

         UOCV = Umeas1 - cell * 0.0002 V * (temp - 27)

The test decision for the open circuit voltage is made on the basis of this temperature-
compensated calculated value. The voltage measured at the end of the test after the high rate
discharge is also temperature-compensated, with reference to the temperature value measured at
the beginning of the test:

         UHRD = Umeas2 - cell * 0.004 V * (temp - 27)

15.1.3.4 Calculation of the Acid Density

From the measured open circuit voltage Umeas it is possible to calculate approximately the acid
density "d" on the basis of the following equation. The correction factor may also be programmed
individually for each type of battery in the system setting menu:

         Umeas1

d     =  ----------- - 0.84 + 0.0007 * (27 - temp)

         Cell

Signification of the abbreviations:

d        =                   calculated acid density of the battery

Umeas1   =                   measured open circuit voltage of the battery

Umeas2   =                   measured battery voltage after high rate discharge

Cell     =                   number of battery cells

                             (< 8V cell = 3, > 8V cell = 6)

temp     =                   measured temperature of the battery

Battery Manager User Manual          99.3                                        Page 256 of 363
15.1.3.5 Test Evaluation

The parameters fixed in the test program are the basis for the final evaluation of each battery
tested. The test result "accepted" is reached when the following conditions are fulfilled:

       1. the test has been carried out completely, i. e.:

               · no repeated contact failure
               · battery voltage not lower than programmed final voltage
               · no unacceptable deviation of high rate discharge current, i.e.:

                       (Iact - 0.01 * Inom < IHRD < Iact + 0.01 * Inom)

       2. temperature within the temperature limit values

       3. the compensated open circuit voltage "UOCV" lies within the limits of the
               tolerance range for the open circuit voltage

       4. the compensated high rate discharge voltage UHRD at the end of the high rate
               discharge test lies within the tolerance range for the high rate discharge voltage

       5. the tolerance ranges updated by the measured values do not infringe the limit
               values fixed in the program.

After each test passed with the test result "accepted", there will be a new calculation of the
average values as well as of the scatter values and the counters "Batteries Accepted" and
"Batteries Total" will add one count. As regards the definition of the tolerance range and the limit
values, please refer to chapter 15.1.4!

In all cases other than the above, the test result will be rated as "rejected". In such a case, there
will be no new calculation of average and scatter values. One count will be added to the counters
"Batteries Rejected" and "Batteries Total" and depending on the type of failure, the respective
error counter will be increased by one count.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 257 of 363
15.1.4 Continuous Update of Comparison Data and Monitoring of Limit
         Values during Production

15.1.4.1 Statistical Calculations

The average value and the standard deviation (scatter) for the open circuit voltage and the high
rate discharge voltage are calculated on the basis of the test results of all batteries with the test
result "accepted". These calculated statistical values allow a better evaluation of the whole batch.
Average value as well as standard deviation are continuously updated after every new test result
"accepted".

       Calculation of Average Value:

                       x  x - 1 n
                                      =
                                         ni
                                                                           i=1

       Calculation of Standard Deviation:

       with:
         = average from all measured quantities xi

       n = number of batteries rated as "accepted"
       xi = actual measuring value of measured quantity x after the i-th measuring
       n-1 = standard deviation of all measured quantities xi

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 258 of 363
15.1.4.2 Description of the Procedure

The average and the scatter n-1 are calculated for every measured value x rated as
"accepted".

The average is encircled by a tolerance range. There are two optional methods to fix the
tolerance range:

       1. Method based on statistical values:

The lower and the upper value of the tolerance range depend on the factor k, the standard
deviation n-1 and the average . The two tolerance values of the tolerance range are calculated
as follows:

                   upper value: Tu = + k n-1

                   lower value: TL = ­ k * n-1

       2. Method based on relative values:

The lower and the upper value of the tolerance range depend on the average and the test factor
k. Both tolerance values of the tolerance range are calculated as follows:

                   upper value: Tu = + k *

                   lower value: TL = ­ k *

The following is valid for both methods:

The new measuring value x must lie within the tolerance range, in order to be rated as "accepted".
If the value x does lie within the tolerance range, a new calculation for both the average as well as
the scatter taking account of the value x.

By these methods, the comparison data (i. e. the tolerance range) are calculated for a measuring
result x from all results rated as "accepted". It might, though, happen that a "gradually
deteriorating" test result will not be recognized as a failure. That is the case, if the calculated
average value and thus the tolerance range is "gradually" drifting upward or downward (see
figure 4).

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 259 of 363
In order to avoid that the tolerance range drifts out of a range that can still be considered as
acceptable, there are additional absolute limits. The tolerance range must to its full extent lie
between the lower limit value LL and the upper limit value Lu.

If the tolerance range is drifting below or above the absolute limit value, the battery will be rated as
"rejected" and the test process will be aborted. The test result will be registered in the registration
data and marked with "Limits".

If that test result appears it indicates that either the limits are too strict or the scatter of the batteries
is too large or that the chosen factor "k" is too high. As, after such a test result, it is necessary that
a new statistical evaluation begins, the PLT will not go into the "Waiting" position, unless a new
test program has been called and started.

Fig. 4: Definition of the Tolerance Range and Limit Values

Precondition for the continuous updating of comparison data is the determination of appropriate
start values for the average and the scatter n-1.

After a change of the battery type, a start value will be determined on the basis of a certain number
of initial tests (= number for average). This number of initial test has to be fixed in the test program.
During this initial phase of a test when the first average is determined, all those batteries will be
rated as "accepted" where the measured values are within the limit values.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 260 of 363
15.1.4.3 Application of the Method on Battery Testing

The method of continuous updating of comparison data is applied to the following measuring
quantities:

temperature-compensated open circuit voltage  UOCV
temperature-compensated final voltage         UHRD
after high rate discharge

A new calculation of comparison data will only take place, if every measuring quantity lies within
the respective tolerance range.

TL UOCV(n) < UOCV(n+1) < Tu UOCV(n) and

TL UHRD(n) < UHRD(n+1) < Tu UHRD(n)

If both conditions as fulfilled, a new calculation of comparison data will be made for every
measuring quantity.

If one of the calculated new tolerance ranges infringes the respective limit value, the battery will be
rated as "rejected" and the test process will be aborted. Hence, a battery will only be rated as
"accepted", if:

LL UOCV < TL UOCV(n+1) and Tu UOCV(n+1) < Lu UOCV

LL UHRD < TL UHRD(n+1) and Tu UHRD(n+1) < Lu UHRD

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                      Page 261 of 363

15.4 Test Program ­ Detailed Explanations

This chapter gives additional explanations on the input of PLT test programs for the standard kinds
of test I constant and U constant.

Polarity
Select the respective type of battery by clicking the button . Select Normal for batteries with
normal polarity and Reverse for batteries with reverse polarity.

Position of Head
Enter here the code number for the positioning of the test head, if your conveyor is provided with
the option for the automatic positioning of the test head. The maximum number of possible test
head positions is 127 (entry 1...127). The code number is transmitted to the conveyor control
through an optional output that will set the required test head position. If your system is not
equipped with the positioning option, just leave the input field blank.

Current
Enter the discharge current for the High-Rate Discharge. For the maximum permitted current
value please see the Technical Data of the Production Line Tester.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                Page 268 of 363
Temperature Minimum (= minimum battery temperature)
If the PLT unit is equipped with a temperature measuring device, a lower limit for the temperature
of the batteries to be tested can be entered here. If the temperature of one of the incoming
batteries is below that value, the battery will be rejected without further test. This means that the
high-rate discharge test will not be performed.

If no minimum temperature is preset, the PLT merely checks that the preset maximum
temperature, if any, is not exceeded.

Abort Voltage (= Final Discharge Voltage)
Enter the permissible final discharge voltage for the high-rate discharge test. If, during the high-rate
discharge test, the voltage drops below this value, the test will be stopped immediately, and test
result U abort will be displayed in the registration data.

Test Time
Enter here the required test time for the High-Rate Discharge Test. The discharge time may be in
the range of 0 to 9999.9 seconds.

When fixing the test time you strictly have to observe the maximum permissible permanent
current for the PLT. Please see Technical Data in the operating instructions for your unit!

Recharge Voltage
If the battery voltage lies between the lower tolerance value of the open circuit voltage
(= minimum) and the value entered as Rech. Volt.: and if, in addition, the test is rated as
Accepted, the battery will be pushed out with the test result Rch.

Temperature Maximum (= maximum battery temperature)
If the PLT unit is equipped with a temperature measuring device, you can enter here an upper limit
value for the temperature of the batteries to be tested. If the temperature of one of the incoming
batteries exceeds this value, the battery will be rejected without being further tested. This means
that the high-rate discharge test will not be performed.

If no maximum temperature is preset, the PLT merely checks that the preset minimum
temperature, if any, is respected.

Entries for Statistical Evaluation
Important Note:
In the following eight input fields you may enter a minimum and maximum value for open-circuit
voltage and high-rate discharge voltage as well as the k-factors for the lower and upper tolerance
range. If no entry is made, no check will be made in this respect.

Preset Values for Open-Circuit Voltage
Enter here the lower and upper open-circuit voltage values for the test as well as the k-factors to
be taken as reference for the evaluation.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 269 of 363
Preset Values for High-Rate Discharge Voltage (U constant: HRD Current)

Enter here the lower and upper high-rate discharge voltage value as well as the reference k-factors
for the evaluation.
A total of four k-factors may be entered here for the statistical evaluation of the measured open-
circuit and high rate discharge voltage values. The k-factors determine the so-called test stringency
for the evaluation.

delta HRDV

For the HRDV difference only a maximum value can be entered. The entry is made in Volts.
Thereby the calculation is delta HDRV = HRDV1 - HRDV2

Begin of statistical evaluation after "n" batteries

Enter here the number of batteries to be taken into account for the average calculation at the
beginning of the test. Entry range: 0 - 99.
The calculation of the tolerance range will start only when the number of batteries entered here has
been tested.
If 0 or 1 is entered, no statistical evaluation will be made.

Sliding Average over "n" batteries

Enter here the number of batteries to be taken into account for the sliding average. The number
should be between 0 and 999.
Only the batteries rated as accepted will be taken into account for the calculation of the sliding
average, as the batteries rated as rejected would negatively influence the averaging.
If 0 or 1 is entered for the number of batteries for averaging, the averaging will be made
continuously.

Registration
Select the registration mode:

All       Measured values will be registered for all batteries tested.
No        No registration of measured values.
Rejected  Measured values will be registered for rejected batteries only.
          There will be no registration of measured data from accepted batteries.

To store your program inputs, click the Save button.

once. You can then use the same button to go back to the single page view.
The Zoom In button enlarges the illustration by a step. You can do the same by clicking anywhere
on the document with the left mouse button when the mouse pointer is a looking glass . If there
is no transition into a looking glass you have reached the greatest magnification step. The Zoom
Out button works just the opposite way. You can do the same by clicking anywhere on the
document with the right mouse button when the mouse pointer is a looking glass .
Use the Close button to exit the Print Preview. In comparison, clicking the button at the top will
terminate the whole evaluation program.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3                                Page 277 of 363
16.3 Nominal Values

Nominal values determine the general conditions for different program steps. The entry in the
program column Nominal Value is made in the following form:
<figure> <unit>, e.g. 500 A or 144 V.
Each entry of nominal value must always be made with value and unit. A different form of entry is
only allowed if a ramp function is programmed. In this case, the entry must be made as follows:
5 - 200 A for an increasing current or 100 - 2 A for a decreasing current. See following program
example.

                         Example: Program lines for a ramp function

If several nominal values for one program step are entered one below the other, the control in the
power unit will ensure that none of the values is exceeded. The following example shows a typical
IU charge.

                         Example: Program lines for an IU charge

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 278 of 363
16.4 Limits

    In all program steps with CHA, RCH or DCH operators you always must enter a
    nominal current, power or resistance! Never enter a voltage value only!

Limits determine the moment for the transition to the next program step. In principle, the following
entries are permitted in the program column Limit (also see chapter 16.1 Program Parameters):

Voltage:      Values in V with one digit after the decimal point                 1)
Current:
Temperature:  Values in A with one digit after the decimal point                 1)

Capacity:     Values in degrees Celsius with one digit after the decimal point:
Energy:
Time:         e.g. 98.5 C or - 30.0 C                                            2)

              Values in Ah with one digit after the decimal point                1)

              Values in kWh with one digit after the decimal point               1)

              Values in hours : minutes : seconds up to maximum "999:59:59h",

              "32767:59h" or "32767h". Time values may as well be entered in the form of

              "30 sec" or "0.5 min".

1)            The number of possible digits after the decimal point

              depends on the technical data of the power unit.

2)            Only if equipped with the respective option!

It is also allowed to set the sign ">" (= greater than) or "<" (= smaller than) as a prefix to the limit
value. If no prefix is entered, ">" (greater than) will always be assumed for program steps with the
operators CHA and RCH, and "<" (smaller than) for program steps with the operator DCH and
PAU. If several limits for one program step are entered one below the other, the one reached first
will trigger the transition to the next program step.

Please note that if Ah or kWH are entered as limit values, these values will be added over the
whole program during charge steps and subtracted during discharge steps. This "balancing"
must, therefore, be taken into account for the entry of capacity and energy values. The capacity
and energy values defined with the SET operator will be taken into account where provided for in
the program. It must, therefore, be ensured that a limit value entered as Ah or kWh is not yet
reached in the preceding program step, as in such a case the program step with that limit would be
skipped.

Battery Manager User Manual            99.3                                      Page 279 of 363
16.4.1 Number of Cycles, Cycle Limit Values

A cycle can only be terminated when entering the number of cycles in column Nominal Value
using "*" or by using a jump step.

is stopped after 10 repetitions.

16.4.2 Fixed Step Times and Program Operation Times

By means of the switch sign "/" it is possible to program fixed step times or fixed program
operation times.
To program a fixed step time enter the "numerical value/time unit" as limit value after the operator
PAU (= pause), e.g. 3 /h. As time units also min or sec are permitted.

                                              Example 1

The entry 3.0 /h in the second line limits the time for the first two program steps to a maximum of
3 h.

With the entries in program lines 3 and 4 the battery is charged to a limit of 200 Ah according to IU
characteristics. When the capacity of 200 Ah is reached, there will be a pause until the total time is
5 hours.

           Make sure that the fixed step time x /h is long enough to ensure that the limit condition of
           the previous program step is reached within this step time.

Further to fixed step times, it is also possible to enter fixed program operation times. This mode of
operation can be programmed with the SET operator and the limit 0 /h. See line 2 in the following
example. The program time meter is set back to zero and the time of the following program steps
will be counted until a limit value entered as x /h is reached that deactivates this mode of
operation. See second last line in the following example.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 280 of 363
                             Example 2

The above example shows:
If an operation time of 5 hours is not exceeded in the program steps 3 and 4, the second last step
will be continued until the limit of 5 /h is reached.
The three program steps with the CHA operator are in this case limited to a total time of 5 hours.

16.4.3 Charge Factor

If certain current quantities are discharged from fully charged batteries, the current quantities
required for a subsequent full charge will be higher. The relation of the current quantity required for
the full charge to the current quantity discharged before (= charge factor) can be taken into
account in program steps with the CHA operator.

                                          Example 1

In the above example, the Ah counter has counted -100 Ah before the start of the second pause
step. If a charge factor of 1.1 shall apply for the subsequent charge step, this must be programmed
by entering the limit value 110 %Ah. In this case the charged current quantity refers to the current
quantity discharged in the last discharge step before.
A programmed charge factor may also apply to several charge steps if the SET operator with the
limit value 0 %Ah has been entered before. This entry will set the Ah counter to 0 Ah and the
current quantities will be added until the next limit value entered as %Ah.

Battery Manager User Manual             99.3  Page 281 of 363
                                              Example 2

In the above example, the Ah counter shows a value of -400 Ah after the discharge step. Due to
the limit value of 110 %Ah (= charge factor 1.1) in the third charge step the battery will be charged
with a total of 440 Ah.

                                              Example 3

Example 3 shows a programming of cycles with the two program steps to be repeated ten times. If
the charge factor is matched for the battery to be tested, the latter will have the same charge level
at the begin of each cycle. Therefore, the cycle times will be constant, although no discharge time
has been entered.

                                              Example 4

Example 4 shows a cyclic repetition of one discharge step followed by charge steps. After the
discharge the Ah counter shows a value of -400 Ah. The limit values 0.0 h and 0.0 %Ah after the
SET operator determine the start conditions for the following charge steps: time counter and Ah
counter are reset to zero. The time of the first charge step until the limit value of 148 Volts is
unknown. This also goes for the second charge step. The latter will only be terminated when the
charge current is less than 5 A or when the charged current quantity in both charge steps has
reached 440 Ah (= charge factor 1.1). The time of the third charge step is automatically limited to
the effect that the total charge time is 5 hours and the time of a cycle 7 hours.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 282 of 363
16.5 Abort Values

If one of the values entered into the program column Abort is reached, the program operation will
be interrupted immediately. It is distinguished between exceeding (marked by ">") and falling
below (marked by "<").

The program operation may be continued after an interruption. This can be done by a new start
command at the host computer or by pressing the start button. If an abort value is reached, this will
be indicated by a message on the screen stating the respective circuit address.

Abort values should always be entered if undesired battery conditions are likely to be expected. In
principle, you may enter into the program column Abort the same values as those allowed as limits
mentioned in chapter 16.4.

16.6 Registration

Values entered into the program column "Registration" determine the conditions under which or
the time when the measured values will be registered. If no entry is made in this column, the
measured values will only be registered at the beginning and at the end of each program step.
This will be the same for program steps within cycles. In principle, the same entries as for the limit
values are allowed as registration conditions.

The most common entry is normally a time value. A time entered as registration condition means a
time interval after which a new registration will be created.

If time intervals are entered for the registration you should observe the length of the respective
program step. If you choose your registration conditions appropriately you will get clear and
informative registration data, avoiding the printout of endless lists of measured data.

An entered time value may also be defined as an absolute value if it is preceded by an equal sign
"=". In this case, an additional registration will be made when the entered time has elapsed since
the start of the program step. If several time values are entered for the same program step with or
without the equal sign "=", the entered time interval will be fixed again when the absolute value
has been reached. See "Example for registration with absolute value" below.

By means of the SET operator, you may enter random start values for the following program step
for the amphour counter and the watthour counter. It is as well possible to take over the
counting from the preceding program step, just by entering the SET operator before the new
program step.

       Example for a program line with "SET" registration condition

In the above example the counter is set to a value of 440 Ah by means of the SET operator, thus
fixing the start value of the internal amphour counter for the following program step.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 283 of 363
                 Example for a program line with "SET" operator

In this example the SET operator is used to take over the counter values from the preceding step
as start values for the next following charge step.

                       Example for registration with absolute value

Registration in the second program step:
1. Registration at the start of the step
2. Registration after 2 minutes
3. Registration after 180 seconds
4. Registration after 5 minutes (= 180 sec + 2 min)
5. Registration after 7 minutes
etc.

16.7 BAFOS Options
16.7.1 Special Operator "POL" (Polarity)

The operator POL (= polarity) serves to reverse the original direction of current, thus allowing a
reversal of the fitting position, as practiced in the "plate formation", in order to prevent corrosion.
The operator POL is normally entered directly before the end-of-program operator STO (= stop),
so that the polarity of the rectifier is reversed for the next lot of plates.
As the operator POL has only a switch function, it is not allowed to enter any further nominal
values, abort values or registration conditions into program lines with that operator.

Battery Manager User Manual  99.3  Page 284 of 363
16.7.2 Polarity Check

The computer permanently monitors the switch position of the charge contactor and indicates an
error, if the switch position does not correspond to the programmed one. Each error message will
immediately stop the program run and this will be indicated in the registration data by entry PER (=
polarity error) under column Fct with the respective registration values.

If a change of polarity is not desired, this error can be acknowledged as follows:

· Press the Halt button on the front plate of the respective circuit or the External Halt button
      and hold it down ...

· press the Start button or External Start button ...

· then release both buttons.

The program will then start again automatically and reverse the stored current direction. This
acknowledgement will be entered into registration data column Fct with QUI.

also click button  to search other reports on your computer. Crystal Reports files have the

extension .rpt. After selecting a report another dialog might appear requesting additional

information. Creating a report can take several minutes depending on the amount of data!

For creating other Crystal Reports based on your data you need additional software, e.g. Crystal
Reports.

Report Events
This report lists events that match certain criteria. After selecting this report you can determine
criteria for it. Choose from:
